Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and other nights I stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. I, um... I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is just... well, my eating has been a little all over the place lately. I mean, it's probably nothing major, just normal stress, right? But some weeks I barely touch dinner at all. And then other nights, I find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone else has gone to bed. It's a little embarrassing to admit, honestly. Is that... is that something that makes sense to bring up?

Well, um... I wouldn't say I intentionally starve myself or anything bad like that. I really do try to eat normally. But... I guess, honestly, that's kind of what finally pushed me to come in to see you. My eating has just been a little... all over the place lately. 

Like, some weeks I just don't have much of an appetite, and I might barely touch my dinner. I don't mean to, I just don't feel hungry. But then, like I was saying before, other nights I'll find myself standing in the kitchen having those crackers and sweets after everyone else has gone to bed. 

It's a little embarrassing to admit that it fluctuates so much. I know I should be more consistent, and I really want to be. I hope you don't think I'm being careless with my health? I'm really trying to figure out why it's been so uneven lately. Does that sound like something we can work on?

Oh, no, it's not like that. I don't go to sleep and then... suddenly wake up just because I need to eat. That sounds a little out of control, doesn't it? 

It's more like... if I happen to wake up to use the restroom, or if I just can't sleep and I'm tossing and turning, then I might realize I'm a tiny bit hungry. But the sleep interrupting comes first, not the food. I promise I'm not setting an alarm to go eat or anything strange like that. I hope that makes it sound a little better? I just don't want you to think I have some kind of terrible eating problem on top of everything else.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... well, I wouldn't say I absolutely *have* to eat just to fall back asleep. I really try not to make it a habit. But... I guess I should just be honest with you, since it's actually what finally pushed me to come in and get some help. 

My eating has just been kind of... all over the place lately. I'm a little embarrassed to admit it, but some weeks I barely touch my dinner at all. And then... well, other nights, I'll find myself just standing in the kitchen, eating crackers and sweets after everyone else has gone to bed. I know it's not a good thing to do, and I'm really trying to be better about it. I just want to get back on a normal track... I hope you don't think too badly of me for that?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, definitely not. I mean... I still eat my regular meals during the day, just like I'm supposed to. Breakfast, lunch, and a sensible dinner. 

Those little snacks at night, they're just... they're really just a tiny sliver of what I eat all day. Just a cracker or two, like I said. I wouldn't want to ruin my diet or do anything unhealthy by eating too much right before bed. That's really bad for your digestion, isn't it? I try very hard to eat most of my food during the day, when I'm active. I think I'm doing pretty well with that, don't you think?

I'm a little embarrassed to admit it, but some weeks I barely touch my dinner at all. And then... well, other nights, I'll find myself just standing in the kitchen, eating crackers and sweets after everyone else has gone to bed. I know it's not a good thing to do, and I'm really trying to be better about it. I just want to get back on a normal track... I hope you don't think too badly of me for that?\n\nDoctor: When you eat these crackers and sweets at night, do you ever feel like you can’t stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I mean... I definitely don't lose control or anything like that. It's really just a few crackers or a small piece of something sweet, and then I make sure to put the box right back in the pantry. I definitely know when to stop. \n\nI really try to be mindful of it, even when I'm up late and feeling a little out of sorts. I wouldn't just keep eating and eating... that would be terrible, wouldn't it? I just have a little bite and then I go wash my hands and head right back to bed. I promise I'm not overdoing it. I hope you believe me? I really am trying to keep things as normal and healthy as I can.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up at night and go to the kitchen, do you feel fully awake the whole time?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes, definitely. I'm completely awake. I mean, I know what I'm doing when I get up. It's not like I'm sleepwalking or in some kind of daze or anything strange like that. \n\nI just... I wake up, and I know I'm awake, and I just go get a little snack and then go right back. I'm very aware of everything. I wouldn't want you to think I'm doing things without realizing it, you know? That sounds so scary. I'm fully conscious and in control... that's a good thing, right?\n\nDoctor: When you eat at night, is it because you feel a strong urge or compulsion to eat, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not a compulsion or anything intense like that. I don't want you to think I'm just... acting on these wild urges. It's really just that I happen to be awake, and I think, \"Oh, maybe a little cracker would be nice.\" \n\nIt's very casual, really. I'm not driven to do it against my will or anything. I just... I decide to have a tiny bite, and that's it. It's perfectly normal to have a small snack if you're up, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm doing things the right way... I'm not doing anything too concerning, am I?\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you eat a large portion of your calories after the evening meal or at night?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I mean... I still eat my regular meals during the day, just like I'm supposed to. Breakfast, lunch, and a sensible dinner. \n\nThose little snacks at night, they're just... they're really just a tiny sliver of what I eat all day. Just a cracker or two, like I said. I wouldn't want to ruin my diet or do anything unhealthy by eating too much right before bed. That's really bad for your digestion, isn't it? I try very hard to eat most of my food during the day, when I'm active. I think I'm doing pretty well with that, don't you think?"
